Turkey to the chairman of the Grand National Assembly There have been major problems in schools for non-Muslims in Turkey in recent years. Although minority rights of education have been guaranteed in the Lausanne Agreement, there have been many problems in recent years, especially when these schools have been wanted to be transformed into trade houses. We propose a parliamentary investigation into the problems facing minority schools. With all due respect. [[İstanbul]] [[Manisa]] [[İzmir]] [[İstanbul]] [[Mersin]] [[Mersin]] [[Ankara]] 8) Cemal Okan Ascendant [[Eskişehir]] [[Scholarship]] [[Zonguldak]] [[İstanbul]] [[Samsun]] [[İstanbul]] [[Amasya]] [[Malatya]] [[40s.]] [[Aydın]] [[Hatay]] [[İstanbul]] [[İzmir]] [[Denizli]] [[Artvin]] The Lausanne Agreement, considered the founding bond of Turkey, contains regulations on the legal and social status of nations considered a minority. In the Lausanne Agreement, the right to education of minorities has also been made. The 40th and 41st provisions of the agreement include provisions that minorities can build their own schools and that the state will provide all kinds of ease for them. In this regard, minority schools have a different way of managing than others. In Turkey, schools have two separate positions in the form of schools and private schools. Minority schools are considered private school status because they are not public schools. However, so far, not one of the schools for non-Muslim minorities has been considered an economic business, given their historical and good circumstances. These schools are exempted from income and institutional taxes, along with the Foundations Act, which took effect in 1936. However, there have been major problems in recent years, as there have been no special status for minority schools. Up until now, student parents have been tried to close the budget deficits, which have been denied admission fees, but this explanation has not been closed with this. With representatives of the community bringing it up, the government has also provided minority schools with the benefit of state aid to all private schools from the 2014-2015 training period under specific criteria. This practice, which has contributed greatly to the closure of the school budget deficit, has caused problems for a short period of time. Minority schools have been assessed as private school status for this assistance, and the Finance Ministry has launched a review. In their study, investigators considered the donations made by some parents as school fees and wrote reports that schools should be an economic business. They can't afford to record students with money from minority schools, one of the most important tools of non-profit and cultural continuity. Many schools will be in danger of shutting down, as many minorities cannot send their children to these schools because minority schools are considered an economic business. Minority schools can only accept students from their own congregations. The fact that the criteria for students being members of the congregation have not been fully determined yet leads to problems. There are also serious difficulties in providing academic staff and lecture material in minority schools. The lack of an relevant faculty for teaching at minority schools can cause problems in the subject. In these schools, Turkish group classes are required by Turkish teachers. This practice is met with reaction by minority teachers and creates a perception that the state doesn't trust them, as well as an inequality between teachers. Because of the civil war, millions of Syrians have had to emigrate to Turkey in recent years. These include Armenian students from Syria. These students are being admitted to Armenian schools. Thousands of Suryani were living in Turkey but were not opening the Suryani school until 2012. Following the cancellation of the National Ministry of Education, which prevents the opening of the Suryani school, the Süryani kindergarten was first opened in 2013. The Suryanian foundations, now preparing to open primary school, are in great financial difficulty. The Lausanne Agreement's exclusive obligations to minority schools include financial support, but the Surarans do not have that support. For these and for similar reasons, non-Muslim minorities living in Turkey are having major problems with education. It would be appropriate for all these problems to be investigated by the Grand National Assembly of Turkey. It has been presented to your knowledge. The motions will be placed on the agenda, and talks on whether or not to open a parliamentary investigation will be held at the appropriate time. Mr. Special, you have a request.

Alex Magaisa, a professor of the Law School of the University of Kent in England following Zimbabwe's politics, referred to the country's desire for centralisation of power because of the transition into the presidential system. Mugabe was not happy with the parliamentary system, he wanted a presidential regime with more power and control. With this system, Parliament has been weakened. Magaisa says the result of the transition to the presidential system is dictatorship, and this dictatorship has existed since then.
